9.2 Background

9.2.1 VNF

The introduction of novel and complex services into modern networks encounters tremendous challenges, encompassing the proprietary nature of existing hardware, the financial burdens associated with accommodating diverse middleboxes, and the scarcity of skilled professionals for seamless integration and maintenance. In response to these multifaceted challenges, NFV has emerged as a transformative solution in next-generation networks such as 5G and beyond 5G, equipped with cutting-edge technologies such as SDN and cloud computing [16]. NFV instigates a paradigm shift in network architecture, capitalizing on virtualization to decouple software from hardware and liberate functionality from geographical constraints, thereby expediting service provisioning [17]. Network functions are realized through software virtualization techniques and executed on industry-standard hardware, as shown in Figure 9.3, facilitating dynamic instantiation without the need for new equipment installations [18]. The strategic adoption of NFV offers a gamut of advantages for network carriers, including reduced capital expenditure, enhanced energy efficiency through appliance consolidation [19], expedited service deployment, and the capacity to tailor services to meet specific customer demands and preferences. Nevertheless, this transition presents complex challenges, such as network performance issues, legacy infrastructure migration, and efficient virtual appliance placement [20].

The key technical requirements for implementing VNF are summarized in the following section.

9.2.1.1 Performance

When implementing virtualized network functions on general-purpose servers, ensuring a minimal performance impact is crucial. Harnessing the clustered VNF instances and modern software technologies such as Linux New API (NAPI)

9.2.1.2 Manageability

In the context of NFV, the NFV infrastructure must be capable of instantiating VNFs at the appropriate locations and times. It must allocate and scale hardware resources dynamically for these functions and establish interconnections to enable service chaining. This dynamic provisioning of services introduces new requirements for managing both virtual and legacy appliances. Managing NFV differs significantly from data center networking, where hardware resources are largely uniform, simplifying their coordination. However, in NFV, resource costs and values can vary remarkably between network points of presence and customers’ premises. Effective management systems must consider these variations and optimize resource utilization across a wide area.

9.2.1.3 Reliability and Stability

For network operators, ensuring reliability is crucial when delivering specific services, whether through physical or virtual network appliances. Regardless of the medium, carriers must guarantee service reliability and adherence to service-level agreements as they transition to NFV. Traditional, purpose-built network equipment in the telecommunications industry has established a reputation for delivering stringent five-nines reliability. To maintain a similar level of reliability, NFV must incorporate resilience into its software, especially when migrating to hardware platforms that are susceptible to errors. Additionally, as previously mentioned, the dynamic nature of service provisioning may necessitate the consolidation and migration of VNFs based on the traffic load and user requests. These operations introduce new potential points of failure, which are required to be addressed through automated means.

9.2.1.4 Security

Security is a paramount factor when deploying VNFs in NFV environments. NFV can introduce new security challenges, as VNFs may operate in data centers that are not owned by network operators and could even be outsourced to third parties. The addition of elements like orchestrators and hypervisors may create additional security vulnerabilities, putting a heavier load on intrusion detection systems. Shared networking and storage resources can introduce security threats, particularly when VNFs share physical resources with other network appliances. Additionally, the integration of software-based components from different vendors can potentially lead to security holes. These changes necessitate a thorough reconsideration of security measures in NFV system design. On a positive note, NFV can also enhance the security of various networking services by simplifying the creation, management, and adjustment of security zones, enabling automated placement of virtualized firewalls, on-demand deployment of software firewalls, and remote updates of security rules for deployed firewalls.

9.2.1.5 Compatibility and Interoperability

A critical concern in NFV is the establishment of standardized interfaces not only among various virtual appliances but also between these virtualized deployments and legacy equipment. NFV aims to promote openness, which means network carriers may need to incorporate and manage servers, hypervisors, and virtual appliances from different vendors within a multi-tenant NFV environment. To ensure their seamless integration, a unified interface is essential to facilitate interoperability. Network operators should also support a smooth transition from proprietary physical appliances to open standard-based virtual solutions since updating all existing services and equipment to NFV may not be feasible. NFV solutions must be developed to be compatible with existing OSS/BSS and EMS/NMS, enabling them to function effectively in a hybrid environment that combines both physical and VNFs.

9.2.2 Blockchain

Blockchain technology operates by utilizing community validation to maintain synchronized ledgers across multiple nodes, as shown in Figure 9.4. While its roots trace back several decades, it gained prominence in recent years alongside Bitcoin. In 2008, an anonymous individual known as Satoshi Nakamoto introduced Bitcoin through a white paper [21]. Bitcoin represents the inaugural decentralized digital currency, addressing trust issues within currency systems. The Bitcoin blockchain, a public, decentralized, peer-validated, and timestamped ledger, chronicles transactions in chronological order, viewable by all participants. Transactions are broadcasted and independently verified by peers, grouped into blocks, and securely linked in a chain-like sequence. Notably, participants can be autonomous agents, expanding possibilities for direct online value transfers, akin to cash payments on the street or instant messaging. Blockchain belongs to the distributed ledger technology family, involving decentralized data sharing across multiple entities. It's important to note that not all distributed ledgers use a chain of blocks, but for simplicity, we refer to blockchain technologies to encompass community consensus-based distributed ledgers.

Beyond its initial design and application, blockchain is a foundational technology that shifts trust from humans to machines and controls from centralized to decentralized [22]. To fully grasp its potential, two perspectives can be applied. First, it serves as an Information Communication & Technology (ICT) for recording ownership of digital and physical assets, along with rights and obligations arising from agreements. Blockchain can record various data, from asset ownership to contractual obligations, copyright information, credit exposure, and digital identities. Second, blockchain functions as an institutional technology for decentralizing governance structures used in coordinating human and economic decision-making [23].

9.2.3 Types of Blockchain

Blockchain technology is utilized for secure transactional and informational exchange within a P2P network. It operates in conjunction with digital cryptocurrencies, serving both public and private networking purposes. In private networks, access is restricted to authorized users, often overseen by network administrators who manage activities and permissions for new nodes or users seeking access. Broadly, there are two main categories of blockchain technology: private and public blockchains, with additional classifications like consortium blockchain and hybrid blockchain based on specific criteria. In all cases, blockchain functions through a cluster of nodes in a P2P network. Each node maintains a replica of the shared ledger, continually updating and verifying transactions, as well as initiating and receiving new transactions. To provide a comprehensive understanding, blockchain technology is categorized into four distinct types [24] which are summarized below:

  1. Public Blockchains: Public blockchains are permissionless and open networks that anyone can connect to and participate in without needing approval. Bitcoin and Ethereum are well-known examples of public blockchains. They offer decentralization, security, and transparency but can be slow and have high energy consumption [21]. Features that this type of blockchain provides are as follows:

    • Decentralization: Public blockchains are typically decentralized, meaning no single entity has control over the network, which enhances security and trust.

    • Permissionless: They are open for anyone to join and participate without requiring permission, which promotes inclusivity.

    • Transparency: All transactions and data on the blockchain are visible to anyone, ensuring trust by maintaining transparency.

    • Proof-of-Work (PoW) or Proof-of-Stake (PoS): These consensus mechanisms are commonly used, with PoW being energy-intensive, as seen in Bitcoin, and PoS offering a more energy-efficient alternative, as in Ethereum 2.0.

  2. Private Blockchains: Private blockchains are permissioned networks having a restricted amount of nodes permitted access. Companies and organizations often employ these blockchains internally. They offer greater control, scalability, and effectiveness at the cost of greater decentralization [25]. This type of blockchain gives the following features:

    • Permissioned Access: Private blockchains enable data privacy and control by limiting access to authorized parties.

    • Scalability: Due to less or fewer members, they are capable of handling more transactions per second than public blockchains.

    • Efficiency: Private blockchains use less energy because they are not required to go through intensive mining activities.

    • Enterprise Use: They are suitable for companies and organizations wishing to maintain data privacy while streamlining operations.

  3. Consortium Blockchains: Consortium blockchains, which include public and private blockchains, are managed by a variety of different entities. This architecture offers a compromise between centralization and decentralization, making it suitable for applications where several parties must safely collaborate [26]. These blockchains offer the following benefits:

    • Collaborative Control: Consortium blockchains strike an agreement between decentralization and control by enabling a collection of dependable organizations to collectively oversee and maintain the network.

    • Interoperability: They frequently make the member organizations compatible, which makes it easier to share secure data.

    • Efficiency: They preserve the level of decentralization while providing scalability and efficiency, much like private blockchains.

  4. Hybrid Blockchains: Public and private blockchain components are combined in hybrid blockchains, enabling functionality to be tuned to specific use cases. They are appropriate for a variety of applications since they are adaptable and versatile [27]. This sort of blockchain gives the following features:

    • Customization: Hybrid blockchains can adapt to specific requirements, enabling organizations to tailor features to their needs.

    • Flexibility: They can switch between public and private modes as per the requirement, which offers versatility in various scenarios.

    • Scalability and Security: Based on the configuration, they can balance scalability with security and decentralization.

9.2.4 Machine Learning

ML represents a prominent subfield of AI, garnering substantial attention and research interest. Two notable definitions of ML, provided by Arthur Samuel and Tom Mitchell, succinctly capture its essence. According to Samuel [28], ML involves computers learning to perform tasks without explicit programming. Mitchell [29] defines ML as the construction of computer programs that improve with experience, using the concept: A computer program learns from experience E concerning some tasks T and performance measure P, where its performance at task T, as measured by P, improves with experience E. These definitions emphasize the core aspect of learning, with distinctions among ML methods based on the process of learning, broadly categorized into supervised, unsupervised, and reinforcement learning.

9.2.5 Types of Machine Learning

ML techniques are classified into the following three major categories:

  1. Supervised learning relies on labeled data for training, optimizing a loss function with an optimizer to perform classification or regression tasks. The process entails collecting a suitable training set, defining input representation, and algorithm structure, running the algorithm to fit the data, optimizing it with validators, and evaluating accuracy.

  2. Unsupervised learning, conversely, operates on unlabeled data, extracting latent patterns. It encompasses tasks such as clustering, dimensionality reduction, and association, employing techniques such as k-means, hierarchical clustering, principal component analysis, and more. Notable examples of unsupervised algorithms include k-means clustering and principal component analysis. Supervised learning tackles regression and classification tasks, mapping input-output pairs to produce real values or categories as outputs. It utilizes algorithms like ANNs, NB, SVM, RF, LR, and LGR.

  3. Reinforcement learning diverges from supervised and unsupervised methods by learning through interaction with an environment, seeking to maximize cumulative expected rewards. It models scenarios as Markov decision processes (MDP), encompassing key terms like agent, state, environment, action, and reward. Reinforcement algorithms include Q-learning, State Action Reward State Action (SARSA), deep Q-learning, actor-critic (AC), deep deterministic policy gradients (DDPGs), and trust region policy optimization (TRPO).

9.3 Related Works

In this section, we will delve into the literature survey and explore the most relevant works in the field of VNF testing using different techniques such as blockchain, ML, and hybrid techniques.

9.3.1 Blockchain-Based VNF Testing Techniques

The cutting-edge and prominent blockchain technology's ability to establish trust among untrusted network participants and its inherent immutability make it valuable in domains beyond financial transactions, where trust and audibility are essential. These properties have prompted research into applying blockchain in the context of VNF security, privacy, and transparency, rather than just VNF management and orchestration.

A recent study by Lal et al. [30] discusses different security threats in NFV infrastructure in 5G and beyond network environments and recommends best practices for mitigation. Different researchers recommended blockchain's potential in addressing security concerns during VNF migration and the research works [31] and [32] showcase various blockchain use cases for secure and trusted VNF service migration and 5G slice provisioning.

In [33] Scheid et al. have investigated that the existing blockchain projects for NFV environments often neglect the VNF repository, which can undermine the trust and transparency of the VNF and introduce BUNKER, a blockchain-based VNF repository that enhances trust and integrity verification for VNF packages without relying on a trusted third party (TTP). It also enables direct acquisition of VNFs through Ethereum Smart Contracts, ensuring vendor payments and integrity verification.

In [34] Nath et al. introduce a blockchain-based testing framework of VNF for trusted VNFs in 5G and B5G networks. It employs both consortium and public blockchains, involving key entities in the testing of VNF and it also utilizes the ability of smart contracts to enhance transparency, security, and trust in VNF lifecycles. This innovative approach addresses issues in centralized VNF management.

In [35] Kaur et al. discuss the significance of NFV as a technology that offers flexibility and cost savings by deploying VNFs on commercial hardware, thus replacing the need for traditional middle boxes. The work focuses on the NFV-MANO framework, comprising NFVO, VNFM, and VIM, with a special emphasis on implementing SFC in the NFV architecture. The study critically evaluates various VIM options, including emulation, virtualization, containerization, and hybrid environments, and proposes a taxonomy for selecting the most suitable VIM, taking into account specific application requirements across different use cases.

Documentation of a solution by Amdocs

The researchers Nag et al. in [36] advocate the use of blockchain technology aligns well with the scenario, particularly in a multi-stakeholder environment involving software and hardware entities hosted by edge nodes in a 5G environment. By utilizing smart contracts deployed on the blockchain, it becomes possible to automate secure agreements between stakeholders, eliminating the need for third-party intermediaries. Additionally, blockchain can facilitate the creation of a marketplace for various VNF suppliers to offer their services to network service providers. This application of blockchain technology addresses security, safety, and transparency concerns in online marketplaces, establishing a trusted environment for both service providers and VNF suppliers. Further, as discussed by Dennis et al. in [37], blockchain-based reputation system can be integrated into the same marketplace and both the service providers and VNF suppliers can identify the reputed stakeholders.

Blockchain technology's trust-building and immutable nature extend its applications beyond financial transactions, notably in VNF security, privacy, and transparency. Research identifies its potential in addressing security issues during VNF migration, with use cases in trusted VNF service migration and 5G slice provisioning. Efforts like “BUNKER” introduce blockchain to VNF repositories, enhancing trust, integrity, and direct VNF acquisition. A blockchain-based VNF testing framework further enhances transparency and security in 5G and B5G networks. Meanwhile, network function virtualization (NFV) offers flexibility by deploying VNFs on commercial hardware, with a focus on service function chains. Amdocs utilizes blockchain to enhance trust in VNF reporting data, offering transparency and security. Last, blockchain's automation and smart contracts streamline agreements and marketplace development for VNF suppliers and service providers, addressing security, safety, and transparency in online marketplaces. A blockchain-based reputation system further strengthens stakeholder identification and trust within these ecosystems.

9.3.2 Machine Learning-Based VNF Testing Techniques

The motivation for employing ML in VNF testing is rooted in the ever-evolving landscape of virtualized network environments. As the complexity of VNFs continues to grow, traditional testing methods face challenges in detecting intricate issues related to security and privacy and ensuring optimal performance; ML brings the promise of more robust and adaptive testing by analyzing vast datasets and identifying patterns that may elude manual testing approaches. This approach allows for enhanced accuracy, efficiency, and adaptability in ensuring the reliability and performance of VNFs in modern network infrastructures.

Adopting agile practices in VNF development enables continuous software delivery, providing a competitive edge to network operators. However, this poses challenges for testing engineers striving to maintain quality in shorter development cycles across diverse cloud environments. ML expedites testing by detecting performance issues in new software builds. Nevertheless, the overhead of managing multiple models for various build types, network configurations, and stack parameters can hinder ML's application.

The researchers Piao, G. et al. in [38] introduce Env2Vec, a deep learning architecture that combines contextual features and historical resource data to characterize stack parameters affecting test execution in an embedding space, enabling predictions for previously unseen environments.

The integration of a single ML model automates error detection and identifies performance bottlenecks in testing workflows. Real testing data yield an accuracy ranging from 86.2% to 100%, with a reduced false alarm rate of 20.9%–38.1% compared to state-of-the-art approaches when reporting performance issues.

Bunyakitanon, M. et al. in the paper [39] provide significant contributions using ML-based techniques for VNF performance prediction. First, they have introduced an innovative approach that augments VNF placement decisions by incorporating ML models specially trained for accurate performance predictions. This approach addresses a notable gap in established orchestrators like Open-Source MANO (OSM) or OpenStack, which lack native support for ML modules. Moreover, the study focuses on predicting end-to-end network or processing delay at the service level, an area with the potential to enhance VNF placement for delay-sensitive use cases. Second, the authors emphasize real testbed-based experimentation, showcasing the practical deployment of Auto-3P in actual network environments, using real data, VNF video transcoders, smart city safety use cases, and real deployment settings. This aspect bridges an essential gap in the predominantly simulation-based literature on VNF placement. Last, the chapter offers a comprehensive comparative study of VNF prediction models and their corresponding placement decisions. They evaluate various ML algorithms and non-ML models while assessing the placement efficiency of MANO when combined with Auto-3P. The results demonstrate significant enhancements in system-wide load balancing and the reduction of end-to-end service-level delay, aligning with the objectives of 5G's Zero-Touch Network and Service Management for delay-sensitive services and applications.

Bunyakitanon et al. in [40] discussed that the autonomous placement of VNFs is essential for ZSM in 5G networks. The researchers introduce AREL3P, an Adapted REinforcement Learning VNF Performance Prediction module for Autonomous VNF Placement, offering two key innovations. First, it uses end-to-end service-level performance predictions for VNF placement. Second, unlike most other ML approaches that use supervised learning, AREL3P employs a form of reinforcement learning adapted for predictions, making placement decisions adaptable and portable to various network conditions. Extensive performance evaluation in a real 5G testbed demonstrates a 40%–45% increase in VNF performance prediction accuracy and an overall improvement in VNF placement efficiency when compared to other benchmarks. These improvements are reflected in near-optimal decision scores in 23 out of 27 scenarios.

In a study [41] by Rahman et al. introduced a proactive ML approach for auto-scaling VNFs in response to changing traffic patterns. The ML classifier learns from past decisions and network traffic behavior, generating scaling decisions in advance. The research explores how different virtualization technologies impact quality of service (QoS) and cost savings. Results indicate promising accuracy for the ML classifier and demonstrate significant QoS improvements and cost savings for network owners and leaders, especially in real-world traffic scenarios and optical backbone networks.

In[42], Zalokostas-Diplas et al. mentioned that the integration of NFV in 5G networks is crucial for flexible resource provisioning based on network demand. The evolution of cloud-native and hypervisor tools enables efficient service monitoring and orchestration for network scalability. These decisions significantly impact user QoS, experience, and energy consumption. ML-driven optimization, predicting future monitored metrics, proactively aids in scaling the network. Researchers explore three candidate solutions (statistical, tree-based, and CNN-based) for scaling network functions in response to user demand. Real testbed evaluation highlights the benefits of ML-driven optimization over existing approaches, emphasizing the importance of enhancing network scalability and performance.

In [43] Mestres et al. conduct an investigation of various ML models and techniques to model complex network elements, particularly VNFs. Researchers focused on characterizing VNF Central Processing Unit (CPU) consumption concerning input traffic characteristics, spanning the transport and application layers in small time batches. By observing CPU consumption from the hypervisor during traffic batch processing, the study empirically demonstrates the ability to learn and model diverse VNF behaviors using ML techniques. The work underscores the importance of applying ML to enhance the understanding and management of VNFs within complex network environments.

The use of ML in VNF testing addresses the evolving landscape of virtualized network environments. It offers the promise of more robust and adaptive testing to detect complex security, privacy, and performance issues in VNFs. By analyzing extensive datasets and identifying elusive patterns, ML enhances accuracy, efficiency, and adaptability, ensuring the reliability and performance of VNFs in modern network infrastructures. The highlighted research papers demonstrate the significance of ML-driven innovations in VNF testing and its potential to improve quality, scalability, and cost savings while addressing the challenges posed by dynamic network environments. These advances underscore the importance of applying ML to enhance network performance, scalability, and management.

9.4 Challenges

Integration of ML and blockchain technologies for VNF testing in 5G networks encounters different challenges. First, the complexity of 5G networks and VNFs demands advanced ML models capable of handling large and diverse datasets for accurate testing. Second, ensuring the security and privacy of sensitive data within the blockchain is critical, as any compromise can have far-reaching consequences. Third, the interoperability between ML and blockchain technologies, along with the integration into existing network infrastructure, requires careful planning and execution. Moreover, the dynamic and open nature of 5G networks necessitates adaptive ML models that can quickly respond to changing conditions. Finally, the need for standardized approaches and best practices in this emerging field is essential to ensure a seamless and effective integration of ML and blockchain technologies for VNF testing in 5G networks.

9.4.1 Standardization Efforts

ETSI established an Industry Specification Group (ISG) for NFV to create a unified architecture for supporting VNF. Key industry players, including American Telephone & Telegraph (AT&T), Bluetooth (BT), China Mobile, Deutsche Telekom, Orange, Telefonica, and Verizon, initiated this ISG, garnering substantial industry support with over 150 members from network operators, equipment vendors, and IT vendors. ETSI's NFV ISG comprises multiple working groups and expert groups, addressing areas like infrastructure architecture, management, orchestration, software architecture, security, and performance. It aims to define requirements that network operators can adapt for commercial deployment, coordinating PoCs to demonstrate NFV's viability. The NFV white paper [17] and related specifications [44, 45] from this ISG underpin parts of this article.

9.4.2 Trust and Transparency Management

Proper trust and transparency management techniques are crucial and sensitive for VNF testing in 5G and beyond networks which includes the following reasons:

  • Ensuring Security: The security of VNFs is essential since 5G and beyond networks are open ecosystems, meaning they play a crucial role in a variety of applications, including autonomous vehicles, telemedicine, and critical infrastructure; the security of VNFs is vital. Trust and transparency management ensures that VNFs operate as intended and are possibly free from vulnerabilities or malicious alterations. It is crucial for maintaining the confidentiality, integrity, and availability of network services.

  • Privacy Protected: Trust and transparency methods protect privacy in networks that handle sensitive data, such as financial or healthcare data. These methods protect sensitive information processed by VNFs from being misused or exposed, and they can fend off various threats, maintaining user confidence and legal compliance.

  • Reliability in Service: Management of trust and transparency aids in quickly detecting and resolving problems with VNFs. In order to provide high-quality network services, eliminate disruptions, and guarantee seamless connectivity—which is especially important in 5G networks serving mission-critical applications—reliable VNFs are a necessity.

  • Preventing Unauthorized Access: VNFs’ legitimacy may be confirmed via transparent management mechanisms, preventing them from being compromised by unauthorized parties. It is essential to do this to stop unauthorized entry or manipulation, which can impair network performance and result in security breaches.

  • Regulatory Compliance: Specific rules and requirements for data protection and network security are present in many sectors and jurisdictions. Organizations can comply with these rules and show compliance by using proper trust and transparency management, which helps them avoid negative legal and financial repercussions.

  • Network Performance Optimization: Transparency in VNF testing allows for the identification of bottlenecks, inefficiencies, or performance issues. This information can lead to optimizations and improvements in VNFs, enhancing network performance and user experience.

  • Business Reputation: Maintaining a positive business reputation depends on trust. User confidence, customer pleasure, and a dominant position on the market are all enhanced by ensuring that VNFs function openly and dependably.

Proper trust and transparency management techniques are essential for maintaining the security, privacy, reliability, and performance of VNFs in 5G and beyond networks. They help protect sensitive data, ensure compliance with regulations, and enhance the overall user experience, which is crucial in networks supporting a wide range of applications and services.

9.4.3 Trade-Offs

Although the use of blockchain and ML approaches for VNF testing in 5G and beyond networks has many advantages, there are also trade-offs in terms of complexity, utilization of resources, data privacy, scalability, and compliance. To determine the potential benefits and cost-effectiveness of implementing these technologies, organizations must carefully evaluate their distinctive needs and constraints. The possible trade-offs are summarized below:

  • Complexity: Implementing both blockchain and ML in VNF testing introduces complexity to the testing environment. This complexity can be challenging to manage and may require specialized expertise.

  • Resource Intensive: To train models, it needs a lot of data and extensive computational power, which can result in cost increases or performance bottlenecks in scenarios with limited resources.

  • Data Privacy: The transparency of blockchain may expose sensitive data in the testing process to unauthorized parties. Ensuring data privacy while maintaining transparency is a delicate balance.

  • Scalability: As 5G networks are built to handle a massive number of devices and services, ensuring the scalability of blockchain and ML solutions can be a significant challenge.

  • Latency: ML algorithms may introduce processing latency in the testing process, which can be a concern in real-time applications that require low latency, such as autonomous vehicles or telemedicine.

  • Integration: Integrating blockchain and ML solutions with existing VNF testing frameworks can be complex and may require modifications to legacy systems.

  • Regulatory Compliance: The use of blockchain and ML techniques may introduce regulatory challenges, particularly in industries with strict compliance requirements, such as healthcare or finance.

  • Energy Consumption: The resource-intensive nature of ML can lead to increased energy consumption, which may not be sustainable in some scenarios.

  • Cost: Implementing and maintaining both blockchain and ML solutions can be costly, especially for small organizations or in situations with budget constraints.

9.5 Discussion and Future Research Directions

In the open ecosystem such as 5G and beyond networks which have many sensitive and diverse applications, the VNF testing process plays a vital role. The application is required to be bug-free and data shared among the different VNFs are intact. To achieve data privacy and build trust, transparency and reputation of these VNFs are crucial. Different researchers have discussed and demonstrated the potential of cutting-edge technologies such as blockchain and ML for achieving this. Moreover, the following are potential future research directions for VNF testing in 5G and beyond networks employing blockchain and ML techniques.

9.5.1 Enhanced Security and Privacy

To protect VNF testing data stored on the blockchain, research can further focus on creating and deploying sophisticated cryptographic methods. Furthermore, it is possible to investigate privacy-preserving ML algorithms to safeguard delicate data while enabling useful analysis.

9.5.2 Interoperability

To enhance the interoperability of diverse VNF testing environments and platforms, future research can concentrate on standardizing communication protocols and interfaces across different platforms. This will facilitate easy data transmission across many platforms and improve teamwork. Investigate the application of ML techniques to dynamically distribute testing resources according to the demands of VNFs and workloads while optimizing resource allocation. Such resource management can increase effectiveness.

9.5.3 Real-Time Testing

Create approaches that allow for the use of blockchain and ML in real-time VNF testing. This is crucial for applications like telemedicine and autonomous vehicles that demand quick decision-making and low-latency testing. Researchers can look into ways to make VNF testing platforms more robust and resilient, particularly when there are network problems or security breaches. This includes adaptable ML algorithms and fault-tolerant blockchain systems.

9.5.4 Energy Efficiency

To reduce the environmental impact of VNF testing processes and address the growing concern about energy usage in expansive 5G networks, research can concentrate on building energy-efficient ML models and optimizing blockchain consensus mechanisms.

9.5.5 Edge and Fog Computing

For decentralized VNF testing, consider integrating blockchain and ML at the edge and fog computing levels. By enabling localized testing and real-time decision-making, these technologies help lower latency and network congestion.

9.5.6 Scalability of a Blockchain

For blockchain to scale to the needs of massive 5G networks, addressing scalability is a major challenge. To manage heavy VNF testing loads, researchers may develop new consensus mechanisms, sharing schemes, and partitioning algorithms.

9.5.7 Network Slicing Integration

Investigate how network slicing in 5G networks can be used to seamlessly integrate blockchain and ML approaches. This can make it possible for VNF testing to be dynamic and adapted to certain network slices for a variety of applications.

9.5.8 AI-Driven Testing

For cognitive VNF testing, research can examine the merging of ML with artificial AI. For more sophisticated testing and diagnosis, AI skills like speech recognition and natural language processing can be used for building the reputations of the VNF.

9.6 Conclusion

In this study, we have thoroughly investigated the fundamentals of the generational progression of wireless networks. VNF, VNF testing, blockchain technology, ML, and the use of ML and blockchain-based approaches for VNF testing we've covered in-depth. We have carefully examined some of the most important existing research in the areas of VNF testing.

Additionally, we looked into various challenges associated with the testing processes as an aspect of our study. We have meticulously taken into account all of the challenges that these testing techniques pose. We concluded our work by summarizing our findings and recommendations for further study.

Chapter 10Blockchain Technology Solutions for Smart Agriculture

Deepak Varadam and Lokaiah Pullagura

DOI: 10.1201/9781003449515-10

10.1 Introduction

Industry 4.0 stands to promote blockchain technology. All sorts of enterprises in the current state use the blockchain methodology to improve security, privacy, and data transparency. Industry 4.0 is a blend of contemporary manufacturing means that aid companies in achieving their objectives more swiftly. Research has recently focused on the potential impact of a number of Industry 4.0 skills that includes Artificial Intelligence (AI), IoT, big data, and blockchain technologies. These technologies give the industrial and various supply chain sectors a wide choice of possibilities. Understanding blockchain and its application in the present climate is crucial for the effectual implementation of Industry 4.0. There are potential uses for blockchain technology in various sectors, including financial transactions, smart agriculture, cybersecurity, healthcare, logistics, etc., where it may boost confidence [1]. The product itself and the unique part of its assembly can both be connected to other blockchain claims in Industry 4.0. It can highlight circumstances to recognize faulty items. All of the products data, including its parts, various distribution methods, and its sub-assemblies are safeguarded by blockchain technology. Costs and interruptions associated with retrieval are reduced across the whole supply chain. The blockchain networks are created with the help of various hardware like camera, sensors, etc. It gives us access to more knowledge than a person could ever. To maintain end-user support, an organization's structure must adapt in a corresponding way. Blockchain is one of the most significant technology developments in many different businesses [2]. Numerous industries, including smart healthcare, smart manufacturing, smart agriculture, smart cities, smart energy, smart transportation, and many more, the usage of IoT solutions has been expanding significantly. A decentralized and trust-less ecosystem is fostered by the integration of blockchain, which enables secure and verifiable data exchange across several parties, including manufacturers, suppliers, customers, and service providers.

10.1.1 IoT

The use of IoT technology in industrial contexts is represented to as “Industrial IoT (IIoT)” robotics, AI, big data analytics, intelligent sensors, actuators, and cutting-edge communication protocols are just a few of the cutting-edge technologies that are incorporated into conventional industrial settings. IIoT is generating next-generation smart structures by executing digital revolution in well-established sectors. IIoT substantially changed and enhanced industrial processes over the usage of technology and the overview of cutting-edge business models [3]. The blockchain method is providing ways to solve the aforementioned problems in the IIoT network. A blockchain is a distributed ledger that enables transactions to be completed and confirmed without the involvement of a third party by using decentralized consensus processes. All transactions on the blockchain are irrevocable since every authorized node has access to the transaction data. Additionally, the blocks of the blockchain are preserved thanks to cryptographic methods including digital signatures, encryption algorithms, and hashing. All authorized users may track transactions on the blockchain, assuring the dependability of the IIoT systems [4].

10.1.2 Blockchain

To enhance the security of big data- and IoT-based applications, blockchain technology is one of the most preferred ways in the present years. The blockchain was first introduced by “Satoshi Nakamoto” via bitcoin. It was initially used only to avoid spending on a transaction twice. However, now it is being used in fields such as agriculture, healthcare, and transportation more popularly [5]. The blockchain makes use of a shared, decentralized, and distributed ledger to ensure data security of the sensors that deal with real-time data. IoT and blockchain technologies are integrated together to overcome many obstacles and is actually a challenging job. A three-tier secure architecture called “Blockchain in IoT” (BITS) has also been proposed to ensure greater privacy and security [6]. Blockchain technology handles security solutions based upon auditability, data storage, traceability, robustness, latency in the work [7]. The paradigm “Blockchain in IoT” (BIoT) is often used in the security world of IoT using blockchain, where the main aim is to integrate the sensor data in the transactions of the blockchain [8]. The cryptographic algorithm “GOST” is used in various commercial enterprise applications. The hashing function GOST is best suited for devices for limited computational devices as it provides the lightweight GOST cipher [9]. In another approach, to significantly save the communication bandwidth and CPU time for the IoT devices, a blockchain proxy can be introduced. The blockchain proxy needs a proxy software development kit on the device that is slim. This proxy has its own private key holds regular blockchain identity. Unlike the standard cyber security framework where humans are responsible for ensuring the security of the entire computer network, in blockchain technology it's decentralized and distributed design ensures everything is automatic [10]. Better data security is provided by blockchain as compared to traditional systems as authorities can manipulate the data in traditional systems whereas in blockchain, data being stored in immutable manner, it cannot be changed. The blockchain also provides a contract-based, secure charging mechanism for the electronic vehicles that maybe used in the logistics framework. The blockchain is based on “Distributed Ledger Technology” which is a way of handling distributed applications really well as required in case of any system. In a few cases, a blockchain system with permission energy is implemented to provide a secure transmission for all the transactions [11–13]. Some of the advantages of using blockchain for IoT are summarized below.

10.1.2.1 Blockchain Stores Data Coming from IoT Devices

Blockchain provides facility to store and transmit the data, in all the layers of an IoT application. IoT along with big data applications in various industries consists of a cluster of heterogeneous devices that are interconnected and controlled by various external devices. In many cases, this cluster may be connected to a cloud, so that the applications may be accessed remotely. Considering such a wide range of data movement, for data storage and prevention of data misuse, blockchain can be considered.

10.1.2.2 Secure Data Storage of Blockchain with Distributed Nature

IoT architecture favors the distributed nature of blockchain in terms of secure operations. In the case of cloud-based applications, IoT architecture faces the risk of a single point of failure in terms of data storage. This can be overcome by blockchain since it stores the data in a secure manner irrespective of the distance between various IoT devices [14].

10.1.2.3 Verification by Miners and Hash Key Data Encryption

The blockchain technology stores only the hash key which is 256 bits of the corresponding data associated with it. The original data can reside on the cloud or elsewhere. Any changes made to the original data are reflected only to the hash key. This mechanism ensures that the data is secure and private. Any data that is stored on the big data applications can be accessed by making use of the hash key. Miners verifies the data that needs to be stored on the blockchain, hence ensuring no malicious data is stored.

10.1.2.4 Prevention against Spoofing Attacks Using Blockchain

When a new malicious node tries to duplicate the behavior of other legitimate nodes on the network, spoofing attacks occur. These malicious nodes can inject wrong logistics data thereby hampering the running of business. However, with blockchain technology, it is necessary for every IoT device to be registered on the blockchain. These devices can authenticate each other, without the need for any broker because of blockchain. The data loss issue from these low-powered nodes is also handled because once the block is added, it cannot be removed.

10.1.2.5 Prevention of Unauthorized Access Using Blockchain

The communication in IoT and big data applications with blockchain takes place using encrypted public and private keys. This ensures only the intended recipient receives the data. However, even if any unintended recipient receives the data, they will be unable to read the data as it is encrypted with keys.

10.1.2.6 Support for Resource-Constrained Devices Using Proxy-Based Architecture of Blockchain

It is not possible for IoT devices to store large ledgers as they are highly resource-constrained. Proxy-based architecture enables implementation of blockchain on IoT devices. The proxy servers enable storage of data in encrypted form which can be later downloaded by the clients [15].

10.1.2.7 Blockchain Eliminates the Centralized Cloud Servers

Blockchain makes the network peer-to-peer by eliminating centralized cloud servers thereby enhancing the security of various applications on IoT network. The centralized cloud servers are vulnerable to attack by the hackers. The blockchain eliminates need for this centralized server as the data encrypted by a cryptographic hash function will be distributed among all nodes of the subscribed network. The development of many platforms to facilitate the establishment and use of blockchains, particularly in the execution of smart contracts, has been prompted by the growing interest in these technologies. A blockchain-based application called a smart contract enables automatic discussion and agreement among numerous companies and participants. A particular blockchain technology might not be able to satisfy the needs for all use scenarios due to the wide range of use cases and application areas. Implementing smart contracts in a particular business environment is a strategic decision by selecting the best blockchain platform. Each platform has unique features that make it more or less suitable for use in relation to particular requirements.

The blockchain technology has been in full swing since its inception with the bitcoins and cryptocurrency. This technology has attracted researchers from various fields considering the security it provides at every stage. From the year 2011 onward, the potential applications of bitcoin technology was extended to other applications such as the transportation, smart logistics, smart agriculture, cyber security other than the financial services [16, 17].

10.2 Application of Blockchain in IoT and Big Data for Agriculture

10.2.1 IoT in Agriculture

The worldwide population is growing gradually which poses a huge threat to the agriculture industry as the demand for more food has to be met in the specified time. Due to the recent effects of global warming, extreme weather conditions, climatic changes, and environmental impact have tremendously increased causing drought in the agriculture industry making the food production harder. Logistics and warehouse challenges are a major concern for the current agriculturalists to move their production from their respective farms to port terminals or processing industries without incurring losses [18]. To meet these demands, new technology is bounded to adopt for better results in the agriculture industry.

Internet of Things is the interconnection of physical objects via the Internet. IoT utilizes complex backend systems based on the requirements of the system. IoT and Wireless Sensor Network (WSN) have been able to enhance the productivity and efficiency of the agricultural field. Digital devices like sensors and actuators helped to automate the process by replacing the previously required manual operation. Major challenge was to provide power to these devices where electricity is not readily available [19]. Current research sectors like agriculture, healthcare, smart cities, smart grids, and transportation are primarily based on the IoT Cloud-based technology. Many researchers or industrialists have invested to introduce the newer technologies on the existing solutions for reaping in profits or societal benefits [20]. Growth of IoT in agriculture sector is very rapid resulting the improvement of farm productivity and profitability. To increase the harvest production, several resources are used in agriculture sector. These resources are controlled remotely and are very much economical for the farmers [21, 22]. An autonomous low-cost network is set up called long range networking which is an open-source technology and is extensively used for many applications and research systems.

Smart agriculture incorporates various smart agriculture gadgets for better control and monitoring of the physical environment remotely through communication networks. This helps to preserve resources and maintain sustainable agriculture. Smart agriculture based on IoT technology is a result of combining modern information technology with agriculture aiding the farmers in reduction of farming costs, improve the yield of crops and better irrigation. Reduction of the network latency is achieved by combiningWiFi enabled long-distance network and fog computing. This helps in resourceful connection of the farming lands situated in remote plates. To classify a set of technological and agrifood requirements, the industrial and scientific challenges are portrayed in a clear manner [23].

Advanced sensors are being utilized in smart agriculture to collect real-time data that helps in making effective decisions for better farming results avoiding drought. This is an advanced technique of growing crops and doing agriculture in a sustainable manner. This technology utilizes the sensors and Radio Frequency ID (RFID) tags that monitor plant growth. The growth of the plant is monitored by the sensor when there is a severe or unforeseen change due to temperature, humidity, etc. A scientist or a farmer in any remote place of the world can gain access to this valuable information via an electronic product code that is sent by the RFID tags which is shared across the internet as shown in Figure 10.1. They can then take the necessary steps based on the data received [24].

The goal of agriculture sector is to safeguard that people have access to abundant and nourishing food by enabling real-time agricultural data. The new hybrid architecture of cloud and fog along with IoT known as “Fog Cloud IoT” (IFCIoT) promises to improve agricultural health [25]. The information regarding various agricultural parameters such as rain, pollution level, soil moisture, water level, pest level, and moisture content of soil can be obtained by IoT devices in agricultural land. This localized information is then transferred to the nearby edge servers that analyze and process the data received from these devices. The edge servers process the data from IoT devices to obtain information that can be used to determine the number of pesticides and fertilizers to be used in the agricultural land to preserve the quality of crop yield. The data can also be used to do predict the possible arrangement of water sprinklers in the field. The presence of real-time analytics engine on the edge servers provides a real-time control of the IoT actuators. These IoT actuator devices on the agricultural fields receive information from the edge servers as opposed to uploading and processing all the sensed data onto the cloud by the servers. The alarms are sent to the concerned agricultural authorities by the edge servers. The cloud data centers are further updated on a regular basis by the edge servers with this information. This enables the cloud data center to make decisions for large geographical areas by performing detailed agricultural data analysis.

10.2.2 Security Issues with IoT in Agriculture

Farmers are extremely concerned regarding the breach of data and manipulation of data against them in the case of IoT technology. Countries depending on the agriculture fields as their main source of the economy are susceptible to this kind of cyber attack resulting in chaos in their economic downfall. Major concern of IoT technology is the confidentiality and security of the data. The problem of temporary work is greater in the agricultural sector than any others sectors. One of the largely important activities for humanity is the exploitation of labor in the agriculture field. Irregular immigrants and illicit brokering acts are the basic exploiters of labor and are punished, according to the law [26].

Developing software architectures for IoT entails the communication of a wide variety of components that plays distinctive roles. Due to the involvement of digital devices, the complexity increases for software development modules and lot of security measures has to be taken in consideration [27]. Traffic monitoring is a serious problem in IoT when the number of links is rapidly increasing leading to link failures or irregularity of the transmission causing an unsupervised traffic model. To overcome this concern, machine learning methods are introduced to improve the network performance for better online monitoring [28]. Steady power supply and customized farming procedures are exceptional challenges for the current Information Communication & Technology (ICT) technologies. Both farmers and technicians will have to cooperate for the better deployment of technological infrastructure. To predict suitable temperature, humidity, and soil moisture of crops, data mining technique was applied to analyze the obtained data that is extremely useful in the agriculture sector [29].

To accomplish a high percentage of data security, the data should be well-protected with good backup and recovery mechanisms. All the sensors, aerial sources, and environmental data are to be carefully monitored as data is continuously flowing through various satellites. Safeguarding the data distribution plays a major role in terms of security issues with IoT applications in the agriculture field. But the hackers are always available to misuse and destroy the data which results in the lack of information about data protection in the agriculture field.

10.2.3 Blockchain Solution for Addressing the Security Issues with IoT in Agriculture

The existing issues related to security and performance efficiencies in smart agriculture is tackled by the implementation of blockchain-enabled IoT cloud technologies. The new type of distributed database is in the form of blocks containing various data, transaction details, accounts, etc. in a safe and secure manner. The blocks are unalterable and are very hard to hack or modify producing a trusted source of information to the farmers by using cryptography and hashed pointers in order to carry a secure transaction. This technology enables us to provide more stability in wide areas of the agricultural industry by connecting all the information about the entire life cycle of agricultural events on one platform with maximum transparency, safety, and speed [30]. Figure 10.2 gives a visual insight on the same.

In a developing country like India, where the IoT-based supply chain management of agriculture has widespread scope, it faces equal amount of technical and security challenges. In the world of agriculture based on IoT, several devices such as sensors and actuators are adapted to control and monitor the various crop factors thereby resulting in greater and better yield of the crops. The main motto of having blockchain with IoT in agriculture is to automate the various agricultural processes thereby aiding the farmers to do a better job [31].

10.2.4 Big Data in Agriculture

Increasing demand for food supply in recent years has made industry leaders and policymakers seek support from various upcoming technologies. The first phase of the process is done by IoT devices that collect raw and semi-processed agricultural data directly from the ground. Thereafter the collected data is combined with other data like warehousing, marketing, weather, etc., which is obtained from the cloud by the analysts. As the obtained datasets are extremely big and complex, a model or pattern is developed to forecast the existing issues and formulate predictive algorithms that alert even before an issue occurs. This foundation establishes a crop intelligence platform as a game-changer in the agricultural industry to boost the economy of the country and sort the issues of drought. Big data has been a big boost in recent years resulting in the increase of crop yield from the existing farmlands. The required amount of rainfall, types of crops, fertilizers, etc. makes the farmers predict beforehand in order to plant and harvest for better sustainability and profitability. Big data suggests using the right type and amount of pesticides drastically by reducing its side effects on the current ecosystem. This helps in the increase of crop cultivation by eradicating the insects and other harmful weeds. Right use of farming equipment results in healthy farmland. Choosing the right equipment is helped by keeping the track and availability of the fleet for optimum usage. Supply and demand issues are also regularly monitored by tracking and optimizing transport routes.

IoT, big data, digital devices, satellites, weather stations, farming equipment, laboratories, farmers, government agencies, and agribusinesses are the various sources where agricultural data is generated and collected. This helps in to produce a smooth operational process and superior product quality [32].

10.2.5 Security Issues with Big Data in Agriculture

Success in smart agriculture has ensured that farmers have adequate data to make the correct choices with the help of newer technologies. In order to carry the success of the new technology, the most important point for farmers these days is the safe and secure collection of agriculture data using fewer resources. Many start-ups are emerging these days introducing the newest software solutions to farmers which tremendously increase the production and development of farmland. But the major concern is to deal with the confidentiality and safety of the agricultural data in terms of regulation which is a drawback for the farmers to negotiate with agriculture tech companies producing their own terms.

A new consent is established by diverse parties in the agriculture industry based on the confidentiality and security principles for the collection of agricultural data. The systems should only collect data with the clear consent of the producer and should be transparent. The transactions between the producers and companies should be governed through contracts, which specifies the terms and conditions that are clearly defined in a comprehensible manner [33].

Many farmers fear the manipulation of data, implicating that the collected data should be separated from personal details such as name and location of the farmer. Due to these various security issues, a considerable amount of growers are not investing in this technology simply for the reason of lack of formal standards in privacy and security. Some of the farmers feel distrust among outside influences which adds more fuel to the fire for the involvement of big data technology. These issues hinder the sharing of data and limit the ability of data scientists and farmers to progress further growth of the technology in the agriculture industry. IoT has to confront varying forms of data from different contemporaneous objects through different networks, which is resolved very smoothly in a short time by big data [34].

10.2.6 Blockchain Solution for the Security Issues with Big Data in Agriculture

Using blockchain technology, a straight forward link is established between farmers and consumers by getting instant data related to various agricultural factors. The factors include the crop and soil monitoring data such as the quality of the seeds, weather and wind conditions, control of pesticide and water spray, including the temperature and soil moisture data. The warehousing and distribution data also plays a major role by collecting the mode of transportation, food safety during an inspection, and quality control of the data. Finally, the retail and marketing data is collected to check the bills, taxes, and various compliances during the transactions. This transparency in the supply chain enables the farmers to get the real price for their products using better technology. These impacts project a major increase in the economy, the safety of the food products as well as reduce uncertainty risk to achieve sustainable agricultural development. Cloud computing plays a vital role in the agriculture sector by converging with big data and IoT technologies by gathering information from various users through preconfigured devices producing better service and quality. Usage and maintenance of blockchain-based solutions come with their own costs and risks. In terms of computational and human resources, cost development, operation, and maintaining applications are relatively high in blockchain technology. The increasing issues of food fraud and security across global architecture supply chains can be addressed with greater success with the involvement of these technologies.

To prevent pests, diseases, and other possible threats in agriculture sector, an evolving trend is introduced as smart farming. This technology improves the production of farms and averts threats and diseases. Smart farming proposes the solution of farming and environment management for better production of farmland. This helps in order to prolong and improve life of cultivation of land for efficient farming. Big data and machine learning technology has improved the production of large amount of IoT data to address complex network infrastructure and program flexibility. This further helps in better quality of service for multifarious IoT-driven applications [35].

10.3 Conclusion

Blockchain led to the foundation of bitcoin and has been widely used today. The adoption of blockchain technology to the various IoT and big data applications of healthcare, agriculture, logistics, and many other fields is a promising enhancement that provides advantages to all the actors involved in the process. Security being an important factor in all three applications is addressed gracefully using blockchain technology at various levels. The blockchain has proven to be successful in transforming even a traditional sector like agriculture with its key features such as auditability, anonymity, decentralization, and persistence. It is also shown as a modern technology that promises ubiquitous business transactions among disseminated parties, without the need of mediators. A complete outline of the application of blockchain technology to provide the security challenges in the fields of agriculture is explained in detail with relevant information. Future work can include exploring security aspects of the application of blockchain technology to other fields like finance, banking, insurance, smart appliances, and music.

10.4 Future Work

Exclusively, and all the more significantly, when consolidated, IoT and blockchain particularly when matched with human-made consciousness (AI) and machine learning give considerably more prospects to medicinal, logistics, and agricultural services. Without any friction, IoT and blockchain technologies give way to exchange value-embedded data in ease for safe security operations. Thereafter the collected data is put into action to create a greater significance without any human effort utilizing the AI technology. Artificial intelligence is provided rich data source from multiple stakeholders to study various patterns and techniques. To gain deeper insight as well to recognize numerous scenarios, AI is used for the exploration of data and how best to respond.

The approach to indoor vertical farming reduces the agricultural inputs and further boosts the productivity of successful farming. Blockchain in agriculture are dominated by Asian community researchers to solve various problems. In terms of local and regional scale, an ICT e-agriculture system is incorporated with a blockchain infrastructure as an evolutionary model. Accurate information is accessed on time by implementing blockchain to various users and personnel. The implementation of blockchain will make the data more secure while also maintaining the integrity of the data. Therefore, more and more healthcare, logistics, and agricultural industries will start to emerge and make use of this secured data architecture and develop technologies furthermore that aid not only the people involved. Moreover, securing the data will allow the manufacturers even to enhance the smart devices to collect and analyze more crucial data.
